    "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hi. Thank you for seeing me. Um, well... it's honestly kind of hard to put into words, but I guess what brings me in is that I just can't seem to shake this... this really heavy mix of fear and guilt. It’s like it’s just stuck to me, you know? 

I mean, I’m a building inspector, so I'm used to being on the lookout for problems or hazards on job sites, but this is completely different. It's like I'm just tense all day long, no matter where I am or what I'm doing. I'm just constantly waiting for something else to go wrong. Like I'm bracing for impact every single minute of the day. 

And then on top of that fear, my mind just keeps spinning, and I keep blaming myself for not stopping what happened. I just sit there thinking about how I should have done something differently, how it's my fault for not preventing it, and I just go over and over it in my head. People tell you not to dwell on the past, but I just have this awful dread all the time, and I can't stop beating myself up about it. It’s exhausting, honestly, just carrying all that around from the minute I wake up, just feeling scared and guilty non-stop, and I just... I don't know how to turn it off anymore.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, um... so, it was about four months ago. We were... well, my husband and I bought this older house a few years back. It’s a bit of a fixer-upper, which usually is fine because, you know, with my background inspecting buildings and job sites, I know exactly what to look for. I know what's structurally sound and what isn't, it's literally what I went to school for. And our back porch was just... the wood was completely rotting out. It was totally compromised. So we decided we were going to repair it ourselves to save some money, just doing the work ourselves on the weekends.

And it was a Saturday, the weather was actually really nice, so we decided to finally tackle the floor joists. My husband, he was up on this section we had temporarily braced... and I was the one who set that bracing. I measured it, I secured it, I looked at it and told him it was completely safe to stand on while he pried up the old decking boards. And then... I don't even know exactly how it gave way, but the brace just shifted or snapped, and he fell. 

It wasn't a massive drop, maybe eight or nine feet, but the way he landed... he ended up shattering his leg and fracturing his wrist. And ever since that day—which, like I said, it’s been about four months now—I just can't stop seeing it happen. Because I'm the inspector, you know? I should have known that bracing wasn't adequate. I should have double-checked the load-bearing capacity of that temporary support before I ever let him step foot on it. For four whole months, every single day, I just sit there thinking about how I basically built the trap he fell into, and I just can't forgive myself for it.

Well, it's... it's kind of both, honestly. I mean, the fear is definitely there all the time, just simmering in the background, like this constant low-level hum of anxiety. From the minute I wake up, I'm just on edge, scanning my environment, looking for anything that might be out of place or dangerous. It’s like my brain is just stuck in this hyper-vigilant mode, and I can't turn it off, even when I'm just sitting in my own living room or, you know, going to the grocery store. I'll catch myself looking at the shelving units in the aisles, wondering if they're secured properly, or checking the ceiling tiles to see if there's any water damage that might indicate a structural issue. It's exhausting, just constantly waiting for the other shoe to drop.

But then, yeah, there are definitely times when it spikes and gets so much worse. Like, anytime I have to go near the back porch, obviously, that's a huge trigger. Even just looking out the kitchen window at it makes my chest tight. And work... work is really hard now. I mean, my whole job is inspecting buildings, right? So I'm constantly on job sites, surrounded by scaffolding and temporary bracing and people working at heights. And every time I see someone up on a ladder or walking across a temporary platform, my heart just starts pounding, and I start sweating, and I get this overwhelming feeling of dread that they're going to fall, just like he did. I start second-guessing every single measurement, every single calculation I make. I'll spend three times as long inspecting a simple support structure because I'm terrified I've missed something again. It’s like I don't trust my own eyes or my own training anymore. So, yeah, it's always there, but being around construction or anything that reminds me of the porch... that's when it really hits me hard and I just feel like I can't breathe.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um... well, it definitely stems from the porch, first and foremost. That’s the center of it all, obviously. But... it’s kind of started bleeding into everything else lately, especially how I view myself, you know? Like, my whole identity is wrapped up in being this meticulous, detail-oriented person. That's why I became an inspector in the first place, because I'm supposed to be the one who catches the mistakes before anyone gets hurt. 

But now, I just... I keep thinking that if I missed something so fundamental at my own house, with my own husband's safety on the line, then maybe I'm just not as careful or competent as I always thought I was. It’s like this constant, nagging voice in the back of my head telling me I’m actually terrible at what I do, and that it's only a matter of time before I make another mistake that ruins someone's life. 

And the frustrating part is—and I do this, I actually go back and check—I’ll look at my work reports, my site evaluations from the city over the last few months, and they're completely fine. There's absolutely no drop in my performance on paper, my supervisors haven't brought up any issues, and the reports don't show that I'm missing things or doing a bad job. But I still just can't shake this deep-seated feeling that I'm a fraud, or that I'm completely incompetent. I'll just sit there at my desk, staring at a perfectly clean, approved inspection report that I just wrote, and I'll think, "Well, you thought the bracing on the porch was fine too, didn't you?" 

So, yeah, the guilt is absolutely about my husband and what he went through, but it's also turned into this huge, overwhelming doubt about my entire ability to do my job, or to keep anyone safe at all. It's just really hard to trust myself when the one time it mattered most, I completely failed.

So, yeah, it's always there, but being around construction or anything that reminds me of the porch... that's when it really hits me hard and I just feel like I can't breathe.\n\nDoctor: Is the guilt you feel mostly about what happened with your husband on the porch, or do you find yourself feeling guilty about other things in your life as well?\n\nPatient: Um... well, it definitely stems from the porch, first and foremost. That’s the center of it all, obviously. But... it’s kind of started bleeding into everything else lately, especially how I view myself, you know? Like, my whole identity is wrapped up in being this meticulous, detail-oriented person. That's why I became an inspector in the first place, because I'm supposed to be the one who catches the mistakes before anyone gets hurt. \n\nBut now, I just... I keep thinking that if I missed something so fundamental at my own house, with my own husband's safety on the line, then maybe I'm just not as careful or competent as I always thought I was. It’s like this constant, nagging voice in the back of my head telling me I’m actually terrible at what I do, and that it's only a matter of time before I make another mistake that ruins someone's life. \n\nAnd the frustrating part is—and I do this, I actually go back and check—I’ll look at my work reports, my site evaluations from the city over the last few months, and they're completely fine. There's absolutely no drop in my performance on paper, my supervisors haven't brought up any issues, and the reports don't show that I'm missing things or doing a bad job. But I still just can't shake this deep-seated feeling that I'm a fraud, or that I'm completely incompetent. I'll just sit there at my desk, staring at a perfectly clean, approved inspection report that I just wrote, and I'll think, \"Well, you thought the bracing on the porch was fine too, didn't you?\" \n\nSo, yeah, the guilt is absolutely about my husband and what he went through, but it's also turned into this huge, overwhelming doubt about my entire ability to do my job, or to keep anyone safe at all. It's just really hard to trust myself when the one time it mattered most, I completely failed.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I don't think I'm sitting around feeling guilty about, you know, random unrelated things like forgetting to call a friend back or something like that. It’s... it’s really that I just can't shake this incredibly heavy mix of fear and guilt that stems from that day. It is just constant. 

It’s honestly what brings me in to see you in the first place, because I am just so tense all day long, literally from the moment I wake up. It's like my whole body is just braced, constantly waiting for something else to go wrong. And while I'm waiting for that next disaster to happen, I'm just endlessly blaming myself for not stopping what happened to my husband. I just keep thinking, you know, if I missed that one crucial detail, what else am I missing right now? What other terrible thing is about to happen because I didn't do my job right? 

So it’s just this endless loop of blaming myself for the porch, and then being terrified of what I'm going to mess up next. My shoulders are practically up by my ears all day because of how tense I am, just carrying all this blame and fear around everywhere I go. I'll be sitting in my truck between site visits, gripping the steering wheel, just running through all the ways I should have prevented it, over and over and over. I just can't seem to turn it off or put it down.

    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient experiencing Posttraumatic Stress Disorder, in a diagnostic interview with a\npsychiatrist. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Is this fear of something going wrong present all the time, or are there specific situations or times when it feels worse?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's kind of both, honestly. I mean, the fear is definitely there all the time, just simmering in the background, like this constant low-level hum of anxiety. From the minute I wake up, I'm just on edge, scanning my environment, looking for anything that might be out of place or dangerous. It’s like my brain is just stuck in this hyper-vigilant mode, and I can't turn it off, even when I'm just sitting in my own living room or, you know, going to the grocery store. I'll catch myself looking at the shelving units in the aisles, wondering if they're secured properly, or checking the ceiling tiles to see if there's any water damage that might indicate a structural issue. It's exhausting, just constantly waiting for the other shoe to drop.\n\nBut then, yeah, there are definitely times when it spikes and gets so much worse. Like, anytime I have to go near the back porch, obviously, that's a huge trigger. Even just looking out the kitchen window at it makes my chest tight. And work... work is really hard now. I mean, my whole job is inspecting buildings, right? So I'm constantly on job sites, surrounded by scaffolding and temporary bracing and people working at heights. And every time I see someone up on a ladder or walking across a temporary platform, my heart just starts pounding, and I start sweating, and I get this overwhelming feeling of dread that they're going to fall, just like he did. I start second-guessing every single measurement, every single calculation I make. I'll spend three times as long inspecting a simple support structure because I'm terrified I've missed something again. It’s like I don't trust my own eyes or my own training anymore. So, yeah, it's always there, but being around construction or anything that reminds me of the porch... that's when it really hits me hard and I just feel like I can't breathe.\n\nDoctor: Is the guilt you feel mostly about what happened with your husband on the porch, or do you find yourself feeling guilty about other things in your life as well?\n\nPatient: Um... well, it definitely stems from the porch, first and foremost. That’s the center of it all, obviously. But... it’s kind of started bleeding into everything else lately, especially how I view myself, you know? Like, my whole identity is wrapped up in being this meticulous, detail-oriented person. That's why I became an inspector in the first place, because I'm supposed to be the one who catches the mistakes before anyone gets hurt. \n\nBut now, I just... I keep thinking that if I missed something so fundamental at my own house, with my own husband's safety on the line, then maybe I'm just not as careful or competent as I always thought I was. It’s like this constant, nagging voice in the back of my head telling me I’m actually terrible at what I do, and that it's only a matter of time before I make another mistake that ruins someone's life. \n\nAnd the frustrating part is—and I do this, I actually go back and check—I’ll look at my work reports, my site evaluations from the city over the last few months, and they're completely fine. There's absolutely no drop in my performance on paper, my supervisors haven't brought up any issues, and the reports don't show that I'm missing things or doing a bad job. But I still just can't shake this deep-seated feeling that I'm a fraud, or that I'm completely incompetent. I'll just sit there at my desk, staring at a perfectly clean, approved inspection report that I just wrote, and I'll think, \"Well, you thought the bracing on the porch was fine too, didn't you?\" \n\nSo, yeah, the guilt is absolutely about my husband and what he went through, but it's also turned into this huge, overwhelming doubt about my entire ability to do my job, or to keep anyone safe at all. It's just really hard to trust myself when the one time it mattered most, I completely failed.\n\nDoctor: Is the guilt you feel about your husband's accident something that you think about constantly, or are there other times when you feel guilty about things that have nothing to do with the accident?\n\nPatient: Um, honestly, it really all just comes back to the accident. I don't think I'm sitting around feeling guilty about, you know, random unrelated things like forgetting to call a friend back or something like that. It’s... it’s really that I just can't shake this incredibly heavy mix of fear and guilt that stems from that day. It is just constant. \n\nIt’s honestly what brings me in to see you in the first place, because I am just so tense all day long, literally from the moment I wake up. It's like my whole body is just braced, constantly waiting for something else to go wrong. And while I'm waiting for that next disaster to happen, I'm just endlessly blaming myself for not stopping what happened to my husband. I just keep thinking, you know, if I missed that one crucial detail, what else am I missing right now? What other terrible thing is about to happen because I didn't do my job right? \n\nSo it’s just this endless loop of blaming myself for the porch, and then being terrified of what I'm going to mess up next. My shoulders are practically up by my ears all day because of how tense I am, just carrying all this blame and fear around everywhere I go. I'll be sitting in my truck between site visits, gripping the steering wheel, just running through all the ways I should have prevented it, over and over and over. I just can't seem to turn it off or put it down.\n\n[Last Question]\nHas this guilt over the accident and your self-doubt about your job affected your ability to perform your daily tasks or maintain your relationships?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 31 / construction_or_building_inspector / associates\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"I'm still working, but my inspection reports take longer because I second-guess them, and I've been turning down elevated deck or porch jobs that I used to handle without hesitation.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well... I mean, I'm still doing it. I'm still working. I haven't, like, quit or taken a leave of absence or anything drastic like that, because, you know, we need the income, and I really do love my job—or at least I used to. But... it's definitely affecting *how* I work, absolutely. 

Like I was saying before, the inspection reports just take me so much longer now. It's actually ridiculous. I'll sit down to write up a report for a standard commercial build—something I’ve done literally hundreds of times, something I could usually do in my sleep—and I just... I freeze. I start second-guessing every single note I took. I'll pull up the building codes on my computer, even though I know them by heart, just to double-check, and then I'll triple-check my own math on load-bearing capacities. I'll just stare at the screen, terrified I missed some tiny detail that's going to cause a collapse. So what used to take me maybe forty-five minutes to write up is now taking me two or three hours, and I'm just exhausting myself trying to get through a normal workday. 

And, um, the other thing is... I've actually started turning down certain types of jobs. My dispatcher will call me with a site visit, and if it involves an elevated deck or a porch... I just can't do it. I tell them I'm overbooked or that I have a conflict, which isn't really true, but I just... I can't bring myself to go out there and stand under one or inspect the joists. I used to handle those without even a second thought, you know? It was just part of the routine. But now, even the thought of having to sign off on an elevated structure makes my stomach completely drop. I know it's probably annoying for my coworkers who have to pick up the slack, and I hate being that person, but I just can't take the risk of being wrong again. So, yeah, I'm functioning, I guess, but it's definitely not the same as it was before. Everything just feels so much harder.

Um, no, honestly, not really. I know I sort of touched on this a minute ago, and I don't mean to just keep repeating myself, but it really does all just circle back to that one day. People tell you not to dwell on the past, but I just have this awful dread all the time, and I can't stop beating myself up about it. I just keep thinking, you know, if I missed that one crucial detail, what else am I missing right now? What other terrible thing is about to happen because I didn't do my job right? \n\nSo it’s just this endless loop of blaming myself for the porch, and then being terrified of what I'm going to mess up next. My shoulders are practically up by my ears all day because of how tense I am, just carrying all this blame and fear around everywhere I go. I'll be sitting in my truck between site visits, gripping the steering wheel, just running through all the ways I should have prevented it, over and over and over. I just can't seem to turn it off or put it down.\n\nDoctor: Has this guilt over the accident and your self-doubt about your job affected your ability to perform your daily tasks or maintain your relationships?\n\nPatient: Well... I mean, I'm still doing it. I'm still working. But... it's definitely affecting *how* I work, absolutely. \n\nLike I was saying before, the inspection reports just take me so much longer now. It's actually ridiculous. I'll sit down to write up a report for a standard commercial build—something I’ve done literally hundreds of times, something I could usually do in my sleep—and I just... I freeze. I start second-guessing every single note I took. I'll pull up the building codes on my computer, even though I know them by heart, just to double-check, and then I'll triple-check my own math on load-bearing capacities. I'll just stare at the screen, terrified I missed some tiny detail that's going to cause a collapse. So what used to take me maybe forty-five minutes to write up is now taking me two or three hours, and I'm just exhausting myself trying to get through a normal workday. \n\nAnd, um, the other thing is... I've actually started turning down certain types of jobs. My dispatcher will call me with a site visit, and if it involves an elevated deck or a porch... I just can't do it. I tell them I'm overbooked or that I have a conflict, which isn't really true, but I just... I can't bring myself to go out there and stand under one or inspect the joists. I used to handle those without even a second thought, you know? It was just part of the routine. But now, even the thought of having to sign off on an elevated structure makes my stomach completely drop. I know it's probably annoying for my coworkers who have to pick up the slack, and I hate being that person, but I just can't take the risk of being wrong again. So, yeah, I'm functioning, I guess, but it's definitely not the same as it was before. Everything just feels so much harder.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything else in your life, besides the accident with your husband, that you feel guilty about?\n\nPatient: Um, no, honestly, not really. I know I sort of touched on this a minute ago, and I don't mean to just keep repeating myself, but it really does all just circle back to that one day. I don't have this, like, general guilt about other areas of my life. I'm not sitting around feeling bad about, you know, not calling my sister enough or forgetting someone's birthday or anything like that. \n\nIt’s just... what brings me in to see you, what made me finally pick up the phone and make this appointment, is that I just absolutely cannot shake this heavy, overwhelming mix of fear and guilt about the accident. It is just constantly there, like this dark cloud I can't get out from under. I'm just tense all day long, like my muscles are completely locked up from the minute I wake up, just waiting for something else to go wrong. It's exhausting, honestly. I spend my entire day bracing for the next shoe to drop, and the whole time I'm doing that, I'm just endlessly blaming myself for not stopping what happened to my husband. I just keep thinking about how I should have caught it, how I should have known, how I'm a professional and I still let the person I love most get hurt. \n\nSo, no, it's not really other things... it's just this one massive, terrible thing that I can't get past. The fear and the blame are just so loud that there isn't really room for me to feel guilty about anything else, if that makes sense. I just can't seem to turn off that feeling of waiting for the next disaster.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you experienced any significant changes in your mood, such as feeling sad or hopeless, since the accident happened?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 31 / construction_or_building_inspector / associates\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State\nmanifestation: \"What brings me in is that I can't shake this heavy mix of fear and guilt, like I'm tense all day waiting for something else to go wrong and blaming myself for not stopping what happened.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, it's... I don't know if I'd say sad, exactly, or hopeless like I just want to give up. It's more just... well, like I was saying before, it's just this constant, heavy feeling. I know I keep coming back to this, and I'm sorry to just keep repeating myself, but it really is what brings me in to see you. I just cannot shake this heavy mix of fear and guilt. It's like it's glued to me. 

I wake up in the morning, and before I even get out of bed, before my feet even hit the floor, it's there. My chest is tight, my jaw is clenched, and I'm just tense all day long, just completely braced, waiting for something else to go wrong. It's like I'm on high alert, 24/7, just anticipating the next disaster, the next thing I'm going to miss that's going to hurt someone. And the whole time I'm waiting for that next awful thing to happen, I'm just endlessly, endlessly blaming myself for not stopping what happened to my husband. 

It's just this constant loop playing in my head, you know? "You should have seen it, you should have known, you're an inspector, how could you let this happen?" So it's not so much that I'm sitting around crying all day or feeling hopeless about the future in a depressed kind of way... it's just this overwhelming, suffocating combination of being terrified of the future and feeling completely responsible for the past. It just drains everything out of me. I don't really have room for any other emotions because the fear and the guilt take up absolutely everything.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Thank you for seeing me. Um, well... it's honestly kind of hard to put into words, but I guess what brings me in is that I just can't seem to shake this... this really heavy mix of fear and guilt. It’s like it’s just stuck to me, you know? \n\nI mean, I’m a building inspector, so I'm used to being on the lookout for problems or hazards on job sites, but this is completely different. It's like I'm just tense all day long, no matter where I am or what I'm doing. I'm just constantly waiting for something else to go wrong. Like I'm bracing for impact every single minute of the day. \n\nAnd then on top of that fear, my mind just keeps spinning, and I keep blaming myself for not stopping what happened. I just sit there thinking about how I should have done something differently, how it's my fault for not preventing it, and I just go over and over it in my head. People tell you not to dwell on the past, but I just have this awful dread all the time, and I can't stop beating myself up about it. It’s exhausting, honestly, just carrying all that around from the minute I wake up, just feeling scared and guilty non-stop, and I just... I don't know how to turn it off anymore.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned something happened that you can't stop thinking about, can you tell me what that was?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um... so, it was about four months ago. We were... well, my husband and I bought this older house a few years back. It’s a bit of a fixer-upper, which usually is fine because, you know, with my background inspecting buildings and job sites, I know exactly what to look for. I know what's structurally sound and what isn't, it's literally what I went to school for. And our back porch was just... the wood was completely rotting out. It was totally compromised. So we decided we were going to repair it ourselves to save some money, just doing the work ourselves on the weekends.\n\nAnd it was a Saturday, the weather was actually really nice, so we decided to finally tackle the floor joists. My husband, he was up on this section we had temporarily braced... and I was the one who set that bracing. I measured it, I secured it, I looked at it and told him it was completely safe to stand on while he pried up the old decking boards. And then... I don't even know exactly how it gave way, but the brace just shifted or snapped, and he fell. \n\nIt wasn't a massive drop, maybe eight or nine feet, but the way he landed... he ended up shattering his leg and fracturing his wrist. And ever since that day—which, like I said, it’s been about four months now—I just can't stop seeing it happen. Because I'm the inspector, you know? I should have known that bracing wasn't adequate. I should have double-checked the load-bearing capacity of that temporary support before I ever let him step foot on it. For four whole months, every single day, I just sit there thinking about how I basically built the trap he fell into, and I just can't forgive myself for it.\n\nDoctor: Is this fear of something going wrong present all the time, or are there specific situations or times when it feels worse?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's kind of both, honestly. I mean, the fear is definitely there all the time, just simmering in the background, like this constant low-level hum of anxiety. From the minute I wake up, I'm just on edge, scanning my environment, looking for anything that might be out of place or dangerous. It’s like my brain is just stuck in this hyper-vigilant mode, and I can't turn it off, even when I'm just sitting in my own living room or, you know, going to the grocery store. I'll catch myself looking at the shelving units in the aisles, wondering if they're secured properly, or checking the ceiling tiles to see if there's any water damage that might indicate a structural issue. It's exhausting, just constantly waiting for the other shoe to drop.\n\nBut then, yeah, there are definitely times when it spikes and gets so much worse. Like, anytime I have to go near the back porch, obviously, that's a huge trigger. Even just looking out the kitchen window at it makes my chest tight. And work... work is really hard now. I mean, my whole job is inspecting buildings, right? So I'm constantly on job sites, surrounded by scaffolding and temporary bracing and people working at heights. And every time I see someone up on a ladder or walking across a temporary platform, my heart just starts pounding, and I start sweating, and I get this overwhelming feeling of dread that they're going to fall, just like he did. I start second-guessing every single measurement, every single calculation I make. I'll spend three times as long inspecting a simple support structure because I'm terrified I've missed something again. It’s like I don't trust my own eyes or my own training anymore. So, yeah, it's always there, but being around construction or anything that reminds me of the porch... that's when it really hits me hard and I just feel like I can't breathe.\n\nDoctor: Is the guilt you feel mostly about what happened with your husband on the porch, or do you find yourself feeling guilty about other things in your life as well?\n\nPatient: Um... well, it definitely stems from the porch, first and foremost. That’s the center of it all, obviously. But... it’s kind of started bleeding into everything else lately, especially how I view myself, you know? Like, my whole identity is wrapped up in being this meticulous, detail-oriented person. That's why I became an inspector in the first place, because I'm supposed to be the one who catches the mistakes before anyone gets hurt. \n\nBut now, I just... I keep thinking that if I missed something so fundamental at my own house, with my own husband's safety on the line, then maybe I'm just not as careful or competent as I always thought I was. It’s like this constant, nagging voice in the back of my head telling me I’m actually terrible at what I do, and that it's only a matter of time before I make another mistake that ruins someone's life. \n\nAnd the frustrating part is—and I do this, I actually go back and check—I’ll look at my work reports, my site evaluations from the city over the last few months, and they're completely fine. There's absolutely no drop in my performance on paper, my supervisors haven't brought up any issues, and the reports don't show that I'm missing things or doing a bad job. But I still just can't shake this deep-seated feeling that I'm a fraud, or that I'm completely incompetent. I'll just sit there at my desk, staring at a perfectly clean, approved inspection report that I just wrote, and I'll think, \"Well, you thought the bracing on the porch was fine too, didn't you?\" \n\nSo, yeah, the guilt is absolutely about my husband and what he went through, but it's also turned into this huge, overwhelming doubt about my entire ability to do my job, or to keep anyone safe at all. It's just really hard to trust myself when the one time it mattered most, I completely failed.\n\nDoctor: Is the guilt you feel about your husband's accident something that you think about constantly, or are there other times when you feel guilty about things that have nothing to do with the accident?\n\nPatient: Um, honestly, it really all just comes back to the accident. I don't think I'm sitting around feeling guilty about, you know, random unrelated things like forgetting to call a friend back or something like that. It’s... it’s really that I just can't shake this incredibly heavy mix of fear and guilt that stems from that day. It is just constant. \n\nIt’s honestly what brings me in to see you in the first place, because I am just so tense all day long, literally from the moment I wake up. It's like my whole body is just braced, constantly waiting for something else to go wrong. And while I'm waiting for that next disaster to happen, I'm just endlessly blaming myself for not stopping what happened to my husband. I just keep thinking, you know, if I missed that one crucial detail, what else am I missing right now? What other terrible thing is about to happen because I didn't do my job right? \n\nSo it’s just this endless loop of blaming myself for the porch, and then being terrified of what I'm going to mess up next. My shoulders are practically up by my ears all day because of how tense I am, just carrying all this blame and fear around everywhere I go. I'll be sitting in my truck between site visits, gripping the steering wheel, just running through all the ways I should have prevented it, over and over and over. I just can't seem to turn it off or put it down.\n\nDoctor: Has this guilt over the accident and your self-doubt about your job affected your ability to perform your daily tasks or maintain your relationships?\n\nPatient: Well... I mean, I'm still doing it. I'm still working. I haven't, like, quit or taken a leave of absence or anything drastic like that, because, you know, we need the income, and I really do love my job—or at least I used to. But... it's definitely affecting *how* I work, absolutely. \n\nLike I was saying before, the inspection reports just take me so much longer now. It's actually ridiculous. I'll sit down to write up a report for a standard commercial build—something I’ve done literally hundreds of times, something I could usually do in my sleep—and I just... I freeze. I start second-guessing every single note I took. I'll pull up the building codes on my computer, even though I know them by heart, just to double-check, and then I'll triple-check my own math on load-bearing capacities. I'll just stare at the screen, terrified I missed some tiny detail that's going to cause a collapse. I've actually started turning down certain types of jobs. My dispatcher will call me with a site visit, and if it involves an elevated deck or a porch... I just can't do it. I'll sit down to write up a report for a standard commercial build—something I’ve done literally hundreds of times, something I could usually do in my sleep—and I just... I freeze. I start second-guessing every single note I took. I'll pull up the building codes on my computer, even though I know them by heart, just to double-check, and then I'll triple-check my own math on load-bearing capacities. I'll just stare at the screen, terrified I missed some tiny detail that's going to cause a collapse. So what used to take me maybe forty-five minutes to write up is now taking me two or three hours, and I'm just exhausting myself trying to get through a normal workday. \n\nAnd, um, the other thing is... I used to handle those without even a second thought, you know? It was just part of the routine. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, um... yeah, actually, I have. I mean, I wouldn't say I've stopped talking to people or completely isolated myself, but... my patience is just practically non-existent these days. It's like my fuse is incredibly short, and I just snap before I even realize what I'm doing. 

Like, at work, for example. I deal with contractors all day, right? And usually, you know, they push back on things, they try to cut corners, and it's just part of the job. I used to be able to handle it, just calmly explain the code and move on. But now... if a contractor brushes off a safety issue, even something minor, I just lose it. I mean, I get so angry, so fast. I'll snap at them, raise my voice, and just come down on them way harder than the situation really calls for. It's like, in my head, I'm screaming, "Don't you realize what happens when you ignore this stuff?!" But to them, I just look like I'm completely overreacting to a missing bracket or whatever.

And it's not just at work, either. It's happening at home with my husband, too, which is the absolute worst part. He's been so patient with me, but... if he asks me the same question twice, or if he forgets something small, I just... I snap at him. I'll be so sharp and irritable, and the tone of my voice is just awful. And then, literally two seconds later, I feel so incredibly guilty about it. I mean, he's the one who got hurt, he's the one dealing with the physical recovery, and here I am barking at him over nothing because I'm so wound up all the time. I apologize, of course, and he says he understands, but I hate that I'm doing it. It's just... I'm so tense and exhausted from worrying all day that the second someone needs something from me or pushes back even a little, I just don't have the emotional bandwidth to handle it gracefully anymore. I just explode, and then I spend the rest of the day feeling terrible about it.

  {
    "role": "system",

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.1, F41.1, F33.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Thank you for seeing me. Um, well... it's honestly kind of hard to put into words, but I guess what brings me in is that I just can't seem to shake this... this really heavy mix of fear and guilt. It’s like it’s just stuck to me, you know? \n\nI mean, I’m a building inspector, so I'm used to being on the lookout for problems or hazards on job sites, but this is completely different. It's like I'm just tense all day long, no matter where I am or what I'm doing. I'm just constantly waiting for something else to go wrong. Like I'm bracing for impact every single minute of the day. \n\nAnd then on top of that fear, my mind just keeps spinning, and I keep blaming myself for not stopping what happened. I just sit there thinking about how I should have done something differently, how it's my fault for not preventing it, and I just go over and over it in my head. People tell you not to dwell on the past, but I just have this awful dread all the time, and I can't stop beating myself up about it. It’s exhausting, honestly, just carrying all that around from the minute I wake up, just feeling scared and guilty non-stop, and I just... I don't know how to turn it off anymore.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned something happened that you can't stop thinking about, can you tell me what that was?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um... so, it was about four months ago. We were... well, my husband and I bought this older house a few years back. It’s a bit of a fixer-upper, which usually is fine because, you know, with my background inspecting buildings and job sites, I know exactly what to look for. I know what's structurally sound and what isn't, it's literally what I went to school for. And our back porch was just... the wood was completely rotting out. It was totally compromised. So we decided we were going to repair it ourselves to save some money, just doing the work ourselves on the weekends.\n\nAnd it was a Saturday, the weather was actually really nice, so we decided to finally tackle the floor joists. My husband, he was up on this section we had temporarily braced... and I was the one who set that bracing. I measured it, I secured it, I looked at it and told him it was completely safe to stand on while he pried up the old decking boards. And then... I don't even know exactly how it gave way, but the brace just shifted or snapped, and he fell. \n\nIt wasn't a massive drop, maybe eight or nine feet, but the way he landed... he ended up shattering his leg and fracturing his wrist. And ever since that day—which, like I said, it’s been about four months now—I just can't stop seeing it happen. Because I'm the inspector, you know? I should have known that bracing wasn't adequate. I should have double-checked the load-bearing capacity of that temporary support before I ever let him step foot on it. For four whole months, every single day, I just sit there thinking about how I basically built the trap he fell into, and I just can't forgive myself for it.\n\nDoctor: Is this fear of something going wrong present all the time, or are there specific situations or times when it feels worse?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's kind of both, honestly. I mean, the fear is definitely there all the time, just simmering in the background, like this constant low-level hum of anxiety. From the minute I wake up, I'm just on edge, scanning my environment, looking for anything that might be out of place or dangerous. It’s like my brain is just stuck in this hyper-vigilant mode, and I can't turn it off, even when I'm just sitting in my own living room or, you know, going to the grocery store. I'll catch myself looking at the shelving units in the aisles, wondering if they're secured properly, or checking the ceiling tiles to see if there's any water damage that might indicate a structural issue. It's exhausting, just constantly waiting for the other shoe to drop.\n\nBut then, yeah, there are definitely times when it spikes and gets so much worse. Like, anytime I have to go near the back porch, obviously, that's a huge trigger. Even just looking out the kitchen window at it makes my chest tight. And work... work is really hard now. I mean, my whole job is inspecting buildings, right? So I'm constantly on job sites, surrounded by scaffolding and temporary bracing and people working at heights. And every time I see someone up on a ladder or walking across a temporary platform, my heart just starts pounding, and I start sweating, and I get this overwhelming feeling of dread that they're going to fall, just like he did. I start second-guessing every single measurement, every single calculation I make. I'll spend three times as long inspecting a simple support structure because I'm terrified I've missed something again. It’s like I don't trust my own eyes or my own training anymore. So, yeah, it's always there, but being around construction or anything that reminds me of the porch... that's when it really hits me hard and I just feel like I can't breathe.\n\nDoctor: Is the guilt you feel mostly about what happened with your husband on the porch, or do you find yourself feeling guilty about other things in your life as well?\n\nPatient: Um... well, it definitely stems from the porch, first and foremost. That’s the center of it all, obviously. But... it’s kind of started bleeding into everything else lately, especially how I view myself, you know? Like, my whole identity is wrapped up in being this meticulous, detail-oriented person. That's why I became an inspector in the first place, because I'm supposed to be the one who catches the mistakes before anyone gets hurt. \n\nBut now, I just... I keep thinking that if I missed something so fundamental at my own house, with my own husband's safety on the line, then maybe I'm just not as careful or competent as I always thought I was. It’s like this constant, nagging voice in the back of my head telling me I’m actually terrible at what I do, and that it's only a matter of time before I make another mistake that ruins someone's life. \n\nAnd the frustrating part is—and I do this, I actually go back and check—I’ll look at my work reports, my site evaluations from the city over the last few months, and they're completely fine. There's absolutely no drop in my performance on paper, my supervisors haven't brought up any issues, and the reports don't show that I'm missing things or doing a bad job. But I still just can't shake this deep-seated feeling that I'm a fraud, or that I'm completely incompetent. I'll just sit there at my desk, staring at a perfectly clean, approved inspection report that I just wrote, and I'll think, \"Well, you thought the bracing on the porch was fine too, didn't you?\" \n\nSo, yeah, the guilt is absolutely about my husband and what he went through, but it's also turned into this huge, overwhelming doubt about my entire ability to do my job, or to keep anyone safe at all. It's just really hard to trust myself when the one time it mattered most, I completely failed.\n\nDoctor: Is the guilt you feel about your husband's accident something that you think about constantly, or are there other times when you feel guilty about things that have nothing to do with the accident?\n\nPatient: Um, honestly, it really all just comes back to the accident. I don't think I'm sitting around feeling guilty about, you know, random unrelated things like forgetting to call a friend back or something like that. It’s... it’s really that I just can't shake this incredibly heavy mix of fear and guilt that stems from that day. It is just constant. \n\nIt’s honestly what brings me in to see you in the first place, because I am just so tense all day long, literally from the moment I wake up. It's like my whole body is just braced, constantly waiting for something else to go wrong. And while I'm waiting for that next disaster to happen, I'm just endlessly blaming myself for not stopping what happened to my husband. I just keep thinking, you know, if I missed that one crucial detail, what else am I missing right now? What other terrible thing is about to happen because I didn't do my job right? \n\nSo it’s just this endless loop of blaming myself for the porch, and then being terrified of what I'm going to mess up next. My shoulders are practically up by my ears all day because of how tense I am, just carrying all this blame and fear around everywhere I go. I'll be sitting in my truck between site visits, gripping the steering wheel, just running through all the ways I should have prevented it, over and over and over. I just can't seem to turn it off or put it down.\n\nDoctor: Has this guilt over the accident and your self-doubt about your job affected your ability to perform your daily tasks or maintain your relationships?\n\nPatient: Well... I mean, I'm still doing it. I'm still working. I haven't, like, quit or taken a leave of absence or anything drastic like that, because, you know, we need the income, and I really do love my job—or at least I used to. But... it's definitely affecting *how* I work, absolutely. \n\nLike I was saying before, the inspection reports just take me so much longer now. It's actually ridiculous. I'll sit down to write up a report for a standard commercial build—something I’ve done literally hundreds of times, something I could usually do in my sleep—and I just... I freeze. I start second-guessing every single note I took. I'll pull up the building codes on my computer, even though I know them by heart, just to double-check, and then I'll triple-check my own math on load-bearing capacities. I'll just stare at the screen, terrified I missed some tiny detail that's going to cause a collapse. So what used to take me maybe forty-five minutes to write up is now taking me two or three hours, and I'm just exhausting myself trying to get through a normal workday. \n\nAnd, um, the other thing is... I've actually started turning down certain types of jobs. My dispatcher will call me with a site visit, and if it involves an elevated deck or a porch... I just can't do it. I tell them I'm overbooked or that I have a conflict, which isn't really true, but I just... I can't bring myself to go out there and stand under one or inspect the joists. I used to handle those without even a second thought, you know? It was just part of the routine. But now, even the thought of having to sign off on an elevated structure makes my stomach completely drop. I know it's probably annoying for my coworkers who have to pick up the slack, and I hate being that person, but I just can't take the risk of being wrong again. So, yeah, I'm functioning, I guess, but it's definitely not the same as it was before. Everything just feels so much harder.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything else in your life, besides the accident with your husband, that you feel guilty about?\n\nPatient: Um, no, honestly, not really. I know I sort of touched on this a minute ago, and I don't mean to just keep repeating myself, but it really does all just circle back to that one day. I don't have this, like, general guilt about other areas of my life. I'm not sitting around feeling bad about, you know, not calling my sister enough or forgetting someone's birthday or anything like that. \n\nIt’s just... what brings me in to see you, what made me finally pick up the phone and make this appointment, is that I just absolutely cannot shake this heavy, overwhelming mix of fear and guilt about the accident. It is just constantly there, like this dark cloud I can't get out from under. I'm just tense all day long, like my muscles are completely locked up from the minute I wake up, just waiting for something else to go wrong. It's exhausting, honestly. I spend my entire day bracing for the next shoe to drop, and the whole time I'm doing that, I'm just endlessly blaming myself for not stopping what happened to my husband. I just keep thinking about how I should have caught it, how I should have known, how I'm a professional and I still let the person I love most get hurt. \n\nSo, no, it's not really other things... it's just this one massive, terrible thing that I can't get past. The fear and the blame are just so loud that there isn't really room for me to feel guilty about anything else, if that makes sense. I just can't seem to turn off that feeling of waiting for the next disaster.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant changes in your mood, such as feeling sad or hopeless, since the accident happened?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... I don't know if I'd say sad, exactly, or hopeless like I just want to give up. It's more just... well, like I was saying before, it's just this constant, heavy feeling. I know I keep coming back to this, and I'm sorry to just keep repeating myself, but it really is what brings me in to see you. I just cannot shake this heavy mix of fear and guilt. It's like it's glued to me. \n\nI wake up in the morning, and before I even get out of bed, before my feet even hit the floor, it's there. My chest is tight, my jaw is clenched, and I'm just tense all day long, just completely braced, waiting for something else to go wrong. It's like I'm on high alert, 24/7, just anticipating the next disaster, the next thing I'm going to miss that's going to hurt someone. And the whole time I'm waiting for that next awful thing to happen, I'm just endlessly, endlessly blaming myself for not stopping what happened to my husband. \n\nIt's just this constant loop playing in my head, you know? \"You should have seen it, you should have known, you're an inspector, how could you let this happen?\" So it's not so much that I'm sitting around crying all day or feeling hopeless about the future in a depressed kind of way... it's just this overwhelming, suffocating combination of being terrified of the future and feeling completely responsible for the past. It just drains everything out of me. I don't really have room for any other emotions because the fear and the guilt take up absolutely everything.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships with friends and family since the accident happened?\n\nPatient: Well, um... yeah, actually, I have. I mean, I wouldn't say I've stopped talking to people or completely isolated myself, but... my patience is just practically non-existent these days. It's like my fuse is incredibly short, and I just snap before I even realize what I'm doing. \n\nLike, at work, for example. I deal with contractors all day, right? And usually, you know, they push back on things, they try to cut corners, and it's just part of the job. I used to be able to handle it, just calmly explain the code and move on. But now... if a contractor brushes off a safety issue, even something minor, I just lose it. I mean, I get so angry, so fast. I'll snap at them, raise my voice, and just come down on them way harder than the situation really calls for. It's like, in my head, I'm screaming, \"Don't you realize what happens when you ignore this stuff?!\" But to them, I just look like I'm completely overreacting to a missing bracket or whatever.\n\nAnd it's not just at work, either. It's happening at home with my husband, too, which is the absolute worst part. He's been so patient with me, but... if he asks me the same question twice, or if he forgets something small, I just... I snap at him. I'll be so sharp and irritable, and the tone of my voice is just awful. And then, literally two seconds later, I feel so incredibly guilty about it. I mean, he's the one who got hurt, he's the one dealing with the physical recovery, and here I am barking at him over nothing because I'm so wound up all the time. I apologize, of course, and he says he understands, but I hate that I'm doing it. It's just... I'm so tense and exhausted from worrying all day that the second someone needs something from me or pushes back even a little, I just don't have the emotional bandwidth to handle it gracefully anymore. I just explode, and then I spend the rest of the day feeling terrible about it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, it's not always just being up high on a porch or looking at something elevated, if that's what you mean. It's... it's a lot of things, honestly. Because of my job, I'm around construction sites all day, and there are just so many little things that set it off now. It's not even a conscious fear where I look at something and think it's dangerous. It's like my body just reacts before my brain even catches up. 

Like, the other day, I was just doing a totally standard inspection on a new build, and I was measuring the height of a railing on a staircase. Just a completely routine thing, I've done it a thousand times. And all of a sudden, out of absolutely nowhere, it was like I wasn't even in that house anymore. I could just see it happening all over again. I could see my husband falling, like it was happening right there in front of me, in real time. It was so vivid, I could almost hear the sound of the wood giving way. And my stomach just completely dropped, like that feeling when you're on a roller coaster and you go over the edge, but worse. And my hands started shaking so badly I couldn't even hold the tape measure steady. I had to just stand there for a minute and pretend I was writing something down on my clipboard so the contractor wouldn't notice I was practically having a breakdown over a staircase railing.

And it's not just the visual stuff, either. It's sounds. If I'm on a site and I hear a ladder scrape against the side of a house, or even just the sound of a heavy tool dropping on a wooden floor... it's like a physical shock to my system. My heart just starts pounding out of my chest, and I get that same terrifying flash of him falling. It's so intrusive, you know? I can't stop it from happening. I'll just be going about my day, trying to focus on my job, and then *bam*, I'm right back in that horrible moment, feeling all that panic and guilt all over again. It makes it so incredibly hard to just get through a normal workday when every little sound or routine task might just send me spiraling back to that day.

I don't know if I'd say sad, exactly, or hopeless like I just want to give up. It's more just... well, like I was saying before, it's just this constant, heavy feeling. I know I keep coming back to this, and I'm sorry to just keep repeating myself, but it really is what brings me in to see you. I just cannot shake this heavy mix of fear and guilt. It's like it's glued to me. \n\nI wake up in the morning, and before I even get out of bed, before my feet even hit the floor, it's there. My chest is tight, my jaw is clenched, and I'm just tense all day long, just completely braced, waiting for something else to go wrong. It's like I'm on high alert, 24/7, just anticipating the next disaster, the next thing I'm going to miss that's going to hurt someone. And the whole time I'm waiting for that next awful thing to happen, I'm just endlessly, endlessly blaming myself for not stopping what happened to my husband. \n\nIt's just this constant loop playing in my head, you know? \"You should have seen it, you should have known, you're an inspector, how could you let this happen?\" So it's not so much that I'm sitting around crying all day or feeling hopeless about the future in a depressed kind of way... it's just this overwhelming, suffocating combination of being terrified of the future and feeling completely responsible for the past. It just drains everything out of me. I don't really have room for any other emotions because the fear and the guilt take up absolutely everything.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships with friends and family since the accident happened?\n\nPatient: Well, um... yeah, actually, I have. I mean, I wouldn't say I've stopped talking to people or completely isolated myself, but... my patience is just practically non-existent these days. It's like my fuse is incredibly short, and I just snap before I even realize what I'm doing. \n\nLike, at work, for example. I deal with contractors all day, right? And usually, you know, they push back on things, they try to cut corners, and it's just part of the job. I used to be able to handle it, just calmly explain the code and move on. But now... if a contractor brushes off a safety issue, even something minor, I just lose it. I mean, I get so angry, so fast. I'll snap at them, raise my voice, and just come down on them way harder than the situation really calls for. It's like, in my head, I'm screaming, \"Don't you realize what happens when you ignore this stuff?!\" But to them, I just look like I'm completely overreacting to a missing bracket or whatever.\n\nAnd it's not just at work, either. It's happening at home with my husband, too, which is the absolute worst part. He's been so patient with me, but... if he asks me the same question twice, or if he forgets something small, I just... I snap at him. I'll be so sharp and irritable, and the tone of my voice is just awful. And then, literally two seconds later, I feel so incredibly guilty about it. I mean, he's the one who got hurt, he's the one dealing with the physical recovery, and here I am barking at him over nothing because I'm so wound up all the time. I apologize, of course, and he says he understands, but I hate that I'm doing it. It's just... I'm so tense and exhausted from worrying all day that the second someone needs something from me or pushes back even a little, I just don't have the emotional bandwidth to handle it gracefully anymore. I just explode, and then I spend the rest of the day feeling terrible about it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other situations or objects, besides the porch or elevated structures, that trigger this intense fear or anxiety for you?\n\nPatient: Well, it's not always just being up high on a porch or looking at something elevated, if that's what you mean. It's... it's a lot of things, honestly. Because of my job, I'm around construction sites all day, and there are just so many little things that set it off now. It's not even a conscious fear where I look at something and think it's dangerous. It's like my body just reacts before my brain even catches up. \n\nLike, the other day, I was just doing a totally standard inspection on a new build, and I was measuring the height of a railing on a staircase. Just a completely routine thing, I've done it a thousand times. And all of a sudden, out of absolutely nowhere, it was like I wasn't even in that house anymore. I could just see it happening all over again. I could see my husband falling, like it was happening right there in front of me, in real time. It was so vivid, I could almost hear the sound of the wood giving way. And my stomach just completely dropped, like that feeling when you're on a roller coaster and you go over the edge, but worse. And my hands started shaking so badly I couldn't even hold the tape measure steady. I had to just stand there for a minute and pretend I was writing something down on my clipboard so the contractor wouldn't notice I was practically having a breakdown over a staircase railing.\n\nAnd it's not just the visual stuff, either. It's sounds. If I'm on a site and I hear a ladder scrape against the side of a house, or even just the sound of a heavy tool dropping on a wooden floor... it's like a physical shock to my system. My heart just starts pounding out of my chest, and I get that same terrifying flash of him falling. It's so intrusive, you know? I can't stop it from happening. I'll just be going about my day, trying to focus on my job, and then *bam*, I'm right back in that horrible moment, feeling all that panic and guilt all over again. It makes it so incredibly hard to just get through a normal workday when every little sound or routine task might just send me spiraling back to that day.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Well, um... no, I don't think so. I mean, I know I just said I've been snapping at my husband and having a really short fuse lately, and I feel absolutely terrible about that, but it's not like I'm pulling away from him or hiding in my bedroom all weekend. I still look forward to it all week. It's not like the fear and the guilt have completely wiped out my ability to have a good time or laugh at something funny on the show. 

And as for other activities, I still really like doing my normal hobbies. I like weeding it and watering it and just seeing things grow. I still want to be out there in the dirt. 

And I haven't cancelled plans with my friends or my sister or anything like that, either. We still go to lunch, we still talk on the phone. So, no, I don't think I've lost interest in the things I used to like doing, and I definitely haven't stopped wanting to be around the people I love. It's just that I'm bringing all this extra, exhausting baggage along with me when I do those things now, if that makes sense? I might be more irritable while we're doing them, but the actual enjoyment of the activity itself... no, I haven't noticed that fading. I'm still me in that way, I still want to do all those things.

I mean, he's the one who got hurt, he's the one dealing with the physical recovery, and here I am barking at him over nothing because I'm so wound up all the time. I apologize, of course, and he says he understands, but I hate that I'm doing it. It's just... I'm so tense and exhausted from worrying all day that the second someone needs something from me or pushes back even a little, I just don't have the emotional bandwidth to handle it gracefully anymore. I just explode, and then I spend the rest of the day feeling terrible about it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other situations or objects, besides the porch or elevated structures, that trigger this intense fear or anxiety for you?\n\nPatient: Well, it's not always just being up high on a porch or looking at something elevated, if that's what you mean. It's not even a conscious fear where I look at something and think it's dangerous. I could just see it happening all over again. I could see my husband falling, like it was happening right there in front of me, in real time. I'll just be going about my day, trying to focus on my job, and then *bam*, I'm right back in that horrible moment, feeling all that panic and guilt all over again. It makes it so incredibly hard to just get through a normal workday when every little sound or routine task might just send me spiraling back to that day.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to enjoy activities or spend time with loved ones because of these feelings of fear and guilt?\n\nPatient: Well, um... no, I don't think so. We still do our normal things, and I still really want to be around him. Like, before the accident, we always had this routine on Sunday mornings where we'd make a big breakfast—usually pancakes or waffles, nothing too fancy—and just sit on the couch with our coffee and watch whatever home renovation shows were on TV. Which is kind of funny, I guess, given what I do for a living, watching other people build houses on my day off, but we still do that. And I still really enjoy it. I still look forward to it all week. I like weeding it and watering it and just seeing things grow. I still want to be out there in the dirt. \n\nAnd I haven't cancelled plans with my friends or my sister or anything like that, either. We still go to lunch, we still talk on the phone. I might be more irritable while we're doing them, but the actual enjoyment of the activity itself... no, I haven't noticed that fading. But now... if a contractor brushes off a safety issue, even something minor, I just lose it. I mean, I get so angry, so fast. I'll snap at them, raise my voice, and just come down on them way harder than the situation really calls for. It's like, in my head, I'm screaming, \"Don't you realize what happens when you ignore this stuff?!\" But to them, I just look like I'm completely overreacting to a missing bracket or whatever.\n\nAnd it's not just at work, either. It's happening at home with my husband, too, which is the absolute worst part. He's been so patient with me, but... if he asks me the same question twice, or if he forgets something small, I just... I snap at him. I'll be so sharp and irritable, and the tone of my voice is just awful. And then, literally two seconds later, I feel so incredibly guilty about it. I mean, he's the one who got hurt, he's the one dealing with the physical recovery, and here I am barking at him over nothing because I'm so wound up all the time. I apologize, of course, and he says he understands, but I hate that I'm doing it. It's just... I'm so tense and exhausted from worrying all day that the second someone needs something from me or pushes back even a little, I just don't have the emotional bandwidth to handle it gracefully anymore. I just explode, and then I spend the rest of the day feeling terrible about it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other situations or objects, besides the porch or elevated structures, that trigger this intense fear or anxiety for you?\n\nPatient: Well, it's not always just being up high on a porch or looking at something elevated, if that's what you mean. It's not even a conscious fear where I look at something and think it's dangerous. It's like my body just reacts before my brain even catches up. \n\nLike, the other day, I was just doing a totally standard inspection on a new build, and I was measuring the height of a railing on a staircase. And my hands started shaking so badly I couldn't even hold the tape measure steady. I'll just be going about my day, trying to focus on my job, and then *bam*, I'm right back in that horrible moment, feeling all that panic and guilt all over again. We still do our normal things, and I still really want to be around him. Like, before the accident, we always had this routine on Sunday mornings where we'd make a big breakfast—usually pancakes or waffles, nothing too fancy—and just sit on the couch with our coffee and watch whatever home renovation shows were on TV. Which is kind of funny, I guess, given what I do for a living, watching other people build houses on my day off, but we still do that. And I still really enjoy it. I still look forward to it all week. It's not like the fear and the guilt have completely wiped out my ability to have a good time or laugh at something funny on the show. \n\nAnd as for other activities, I still really like doing my normal hobbies. I have this vegetable garden in the backyard that I started a couple of years ago—just some tomatoes, bell peppers, a little bit of zucchini—and I still get a lot of satisfaction out of going out there. I like weeding it and watering it and just seeing things grow. I might be more irritable while we're doing them, but the actual enjoyment of the activity itself... no, I haven't noticed that fading. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, um, I mean, I'm still getting up every day and doing what I need to do, so I haven't like, quit my job or stopped paying the bills or anything like that. I'm still working. But... it's just that the way I'm doing my job has definitely changed, and it's taking a lot more out of me just to maintain that baseline routine. 

Like, take my inspection reports, for example. I've been doing this a while, so usually, I can just knock those out. I go to the site, take my notes, get back to my truck, and type it all up in the system. It used to be second nature. But lately... oh my gosh, it takes me twice as long to get through a single report. I just sit there staring at the screen, and I start second-guessing absolutely everything I wrote down. I'll think, "Wait, did I check the spacing on those joists properly? Did I really look at the flashing?" And even if I *know* I checked it—even if I have the photos right there on my tablet to prove it—I just obsess over it. I'm so terrified of missing some tiny detail that could lead to a collapse or an accident, you know? So I just read and re-read these reports until my eyes cross, which obviously puts me behind on my schedule for the rest of the day. 

And then, um, there's the actual sites I'm visiting. I'm still doing my routes, but I've actually started turning down certain assignments, which is totally unlike me. If I see that a residential job involves an elevated deck or a big porch... I just can't do it. I've been turning them down. I used to handle those without hesitation—it's just basic bread-and-butter stuff for an inspector, checking the ledger boards and the footings and all that. But now, if I see "elevated deck inspection" on my docket, I just... I make up an excuse. I'll tell my supervisor my route is overloaded, or I'll ask one of the other guys to trade properties with me. I just can't bring myself to stand up there and be the one to sign off on it. The responsibility just feels way too heavy now. So, I guess I'm maintaining things on the surface, but underneath, I'm definitely having to maneuver around all these new roadblocks just to get through the week.

The fear and the blame are just so loud that there isn't really room for me to feel guilty about anything else, if that makes sense. I just can't seem to turn off that feeling of waiting for the next disaster.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant changes in your mood, such as feeling sad or hopeless, since the accident happened?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... I don't know if I'd say sad, exactly, or hopeless like I just want to give up. It's more just... well, like I was saying before, it's just this constant, heavy feeling. I know I keep coming back to this, and I'm sorry to just keep repeating myself, but it really is what brings me in to see you. I just cannot shake this heavy mix of fear and guilt. It's like it's glued to me. \n\nI wake up in the morning, and before I even get out of bed, before my feet even hit the floor, it's there. My chest is tight, my jaw is clenched, and I'm just tense all day long, just completely braced, waiting for something else to go wrong. It's like I'm on high alert, 24/7, just anticipating the next disaster, the next thing I'm going to miss that's going to hurt someone. And the whole time I'm waiting for that next awful thing to happen, I'm just endlessly, endlessly blaming myself for not stopping what happened to my husband. \n\nIt's just this constant loop playing in my head, you know? \"You should have seen it, you should have known, you're an inspector, how could you let this happen?\" So it's not so much that I'm sitting around crying all day or feeling hopeless about the future in a depressed kind of way... it's just this overwhelming, suffocating combination of being terrified of the future and feeling completely responsible for the past. It just drains everything out of me. I don't really have room for any other emotions because the fear and the guilt take up absolutely everything.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships with friends and family since the accident happened?\n\nPatient: Well, um... yeah, actually, I have. I mean, I wouldn't say I've stopped talking to people or completely isolated myself, but... my patience is just practically non-existent these days. It's like my fuse is incredibly short, and I just snap before I even realize what I'm doing. \n\nLike, at work, for example. I deal with contractors all day, right? And usually, you know, they push back on things, they try to cut corners, and it's just part of the job. I used to be able to handle it, just calmly explain the code and move on. But now... if a contractor brushes off a safety issue, even something minor, I just lose it. I mean, I get so angry, so fast. I'll snap at them, raise my voice, and just come down on them way harder than the situation really calls for. It's like my body just reacts before my brain even catches up. \n\nLike, the other day, I was just doing a totally standard inspection on a new build, and I was measuring the height of a railing on a staircase.
